## Page 1


Noncommutative Geometry, Spectral Asymptotics,
and Semiclassical Analysis
Rapha¨el Ponge
School of Mathematics, Sichuan University, Chengdu, China
Email address: ponge.math@icloud.com
arXiv:2604.15008v1  [math.OA]  16 Apr 2026


## Page 2


Abstract. Semiclassical analysis and noncommutative geometry are two pillars of quantum
theory. It’s only recently that bridges between them have been emerging. In this monograph, we
combine various techniques from functional analysis and spectral theory to obtain semiclassical
Weyl laws and extensions of Connes’ integration formula for a large class of noncommutative
manifolds (i.e., spectral triples).
These results generalize and simplify recent results of McDonald-Sukochev-Zanin. In par-
ticular, all the regularity assumptions and restrictions on dimension there are removed in our
approach. Moreover, the Tauberian condition used by McDonald-Sukochev-Zanin is replaced
by a weaker spectral theoretic condition, called Condition (W). That condition holds in fairly
greater generality and significantly open the scope of applicability of the main results.
We also give Tauberian conditions that imply Condition (W). These Tauberian conditions
are easier to check in practice than the Tauberian condition of McDonald-Sukochev-Zanin and
are satisfied in numerous examples. The need for these conditions was highlighted by Alain
Connes in an online seminar.
The main results of this memoire are illustrated by semiclassical Weyl’s laws and integration
formulas in the following settings: (i) Dirichlet and Neumann problems on Euclidean domains
with smooth boundaries; (ii) closed Riemannian manifolds; (iii) open manifolds with conformally
cusp metrics of finite volume; (iv) quantum tori; and (v) sub-Riemannian manifolds.
In the settings (i) and (ii), we recover well known semiclassical Weyl laws, as well as Weyl
laws for Steklov eigenvalues. The only novelty is obtaining them from old results of Carleman,
Minakshisundaram, and Minakshisundaram-Pleijel on heat kernel asymptotics. Moreover, we
get a new proof of the Weyl laws of Birman-Solomyak for negative order pseudodifferential op-
erators. We actually prove the result for suitable closures of the spaces of pseudodifferential
operators. This then leads to a far-reaching refinement of Connes’ trace theorem for (classi-
cal) pseudodifferential operators on closed manifolds. This subsumes its recent extension by
Kordyukov-Sukochev-Zanin.
In the setting (iii), the results are established for fractional powers of the Dirac operator
and magnetic Laplacians, since those operators have discrete spectra. The results in this geo-
metric setting are new. Note that the metrics at stake need not be complete. Therefore, our
semiclassical Weyl laws complement recent semiclassical Weyl laws of Braverman and Dai-Yan
for complete Riemannian manifolds.
In the setting (iv), i.e., quantum tori, the semiclassical Weyl laws provide a positive answer
to a conjecture of Edward McDonald and the author. The integration formulas are refinements
of several previous analogues of Connes’ integration formula for quantum tori.
In the setting (v), i.e., sub-Riemmannian geometry, the semiclassical Weyl’s laws and inte-
gration formulas are new results. The former connect with a recent string of results on spectral
asymptotics and quantum limits in sub-Riemannian geometry. In the case contact manifolds,
the integration formulas are refinements of integration formulas of Kordyukov-Sukochev-Zanin
and the author. The main focus is on equiregular sub-Riemannin structures, but we also discuss
the extension of the results to the singular setting in the special case of the Baouendi-Grushin
example.

CHAPTER 1
Introduction
1. Main Results
Noncommutative geometry and semiclassical analysis are two important fields within the
broader area of quantum theory.
Recently, bridges between them have been emerging (see,
e.g., [137, 156, 157, 165, 192]). This has its roots, on the one hand, in the functional analytic ap-
proach to semiclassical analysis of Schr¨odinger operators pioneered by Birman-Solomyak [21, 30]
and Simon [180], and, on the other hand, in the paramount role of operator ideals in noncommu-
tative geometry program of Connes [53].
In the framework of noncommutative geometry, the role of manifolds is played by spectral
triples (A , H , D), where A is a (unital) ∗-algebra represented by bounded operators on the
Hilbert space H and D is an unbounded selfadjoint operator on H with compact resolvent which
commutes with elements of A up to bounded operators. The notion of dimension is replaced by
p-summability, in the sense that D−1 lies in the weak Schatten class Lp,∞. The main prototype
of a spectral triple is the Dirac spectral triple (C∞(M), L2(M, /S), /D) associated with any closed
Riemannian spin manifold. A closely related example is the square-root Laplacian spectral triple
(C∞(M), L2(M),
p
∆g).
In a recent article [137] McDonald-Sukochev-Zanin established a semiclassical Weyl law for
spectral triples (A , H , D), i.e., for the counting functions of Schr¨odinger operators h2D2 + V
under the semiclassical limit h →0+. This semiclassical Weyl law is established as a consequence
of a general Tauberian theorem for non-commuting pairs of operators on Hilbert space (see [137,
Theorem 1.2]). In order to apply this Tauberian theorem three conditions for the spectral triple
are required:
(a) p-summability with p > 2.
(b) Lipschitz regularity, i.e., [|D|, a] ∈L (H ) for all a ∈A .
(c) A Tauberian condition for the zeta functions Tr[az|D|−z], a ≥0, ℜz > p (see Condi-
tion 4.2 for the precise statement).
It would be desirable to remove the technical conditions (a) and (b), especially the former,
since it prevents us from dealing with 1-dimensional and 2-dimensional examples. It is actually
conjectured in [137] that the main results there should hold for p ≤2.
In addition, it would be good to relate the Tauberian condition (c) to more standard Tauberian
conditions in terms of zeta functions Tr[a|D|−z] or heat traces Tr[ae−tD2], t > 0, which do not
involve powers of a. The need for this was pointed out by Alain Connes during an online seminar
in May 2021. He also pointed that closedness under holomorphic functional calculus should play
a role.
The main aim of this monograph is to show that, in the setup of spectral triples, results
such as semiclassical Weyl laws and Connes’ integration formulas, as well as Weyl laws for Steklov
eigenvalues are mere consequences of classical Weyl laws for some Laplace-type operators, namely,
operators of the form aD2a, where a ranges over positive invertible elements of A . This simplifies
the approach of [137] and leads to stronger results. In particular, we remove the conditions (a)–(b)
in the approach in the approach of [137] and replace the Tauberian condition of [137] by a more
general spectral theoretic condition (see Condition (W) below). That condition is weaker and
easier to check in practice, which allows us to widen significantly the scope of applications of the
results and cover various new examples. We also exhibit Tauberian conditions (Condition (Z) and
1

spectrum), and set A++ = A ∩A ++. Moreover, given any a ∈A ++, we let

In particular, this semiclassical Weyl law holds under the Tauberian conditions (H) and (Z).
For q = 1 we recover from (1.4) the semiclassical Weyl law of [137] without any extra regularity
or requiring p to be > 2. The semiclassical Weyl law (1.4) for q ̸= 1 are new even for p > 2.
It is known that the semiclassical Weyl law for q = 2 implies a classical Weyl law for the
eigenvalue distribution of D2. Therefore, we may interpret Theorem 1.2 as some kind of converse
of this statement.
1.4. Integration formulas. The Weyl laws (1.2)–(1.3) have further implications in non-
commutative geometry. In the framework of noncommutative geometry the role of the integral
is played by positive traces in L1,∞(see [53]). An important class of such traces is provided by
Dixmier traces [69]. Following Connes [53] an operator A ∈L1,∞is called measurable if it takes
the same value on all Dixmier traces. Equivalently, A is measurable if and only if

operators by looking at real and imaginary parts. If these conditions are satisfied we shall say that
A is spectrally measurable. Thus, spectral measurability is an even stronger form of measurability
than strong measurability.
It actually follows from results of Birman-Solomyak [26, 27, 28] that, on any closed manifold
M, the ΨDOs of order −dim M are spectrally measurable. Birman-Solomyak’s result actually
predates Connes’ integration formula (1.5) and implies a stronger form of this result.
In the light of this, the spectral asymptotics (1.2)–(1.3) imply far-reaching extensions of
Connes’ integration formula to our class of spectral triples.

1.5. Extensions to unbounded potentials. It is important to understand to which extent
the spectral asymptotics (1.2)–(1.3), semiclassical Weyl law (1.4) and integration formulas (1.6)–
(1.7) above continue to hold whenever the corresponding r.h.s. make sense. Note that, from a
spectral theoretic perspective, this involves considering unbounded potentials. On Rn or bounded
domains in Rn, the extension of the semiclassical Weyl laws to optimal Lr-spaces of potentials was
made possible by the CLR inequality of Cwikel [64], Lieb [127, 128], and Rozenblum [163, 164].
The approach of Cwikel [64] relied on proving some weak-Schatten quasi-norm estimates for
Birman-Schwinger operators that were conjectured by Simon [180]. Combining these estimates
with the perturbation theory of Birman-Solomyak further enables us to extends the spectral asymp-
totics for eigenvalues of Birman-Schwinger operators and the integration formulas to the relevant
Lr-classes. We refer to [124, 165, 168, 186, 191], and the references therein, for various exten-
sions of the Cwikel estimates.
In this monograph, we lay down a general process for extending to “Lr-potentials” the spectral
asymptotics (1.2)–(1.3). This will then allow us to extend to this class of potential the semiclassical
Weyl law (1.4) and integration formulas (1.6)–(1.7). Conceptually, this is only a mere elaboration
of the approaches of Birman-Solomyak (see, e.g., [24]) and Simon [180] to semiclassical Weyl laws
for Lr-potentials. The only technical difference here is that, as we are working in a noncommutative
setting, we need to work with noncommutative Lr-spaces.
We proceed as follows. Suppose that Condition (W) holds and τ is the restriction of a positive
faithful trace τ : M →C, where M ⊆L (H ) is the von Neumann algebra generated by A (i.e.,
its weak closure in L (H )). As τ is finite, for any r ∈[1, ∞) we may define the noncommutative
Lr-space Lr(M )1 as the closure of M with respect to the Banach norm x →∥x∥r :=

1.6. Tauberian conditions. There are various ways to establish Weyl laws. They are often
deduced from Tauberian theorems. The approach of [137] relies on some new Tauberian condition.
As mentioned above, the need for a more standard Tauberian condition was pointed out by Alain
Connes. From this perspective, we establish the following Tauberian criteria for Condition (W).
Theorem 1.7 (Tauberian Theorem). Assume there exists a Fr´echet subalgebra B ⊆A which
contains A and is closed under holomorphic functional calculus such that one of the following
conditions is satisfied:
• Condition (Z). For every a ∈B, the function
Tr

Then the Weyl law (1.1) is satisfied for all a ∈A++, i.e., Condition (W) holds.
Theorem 1.7 is a refinement of the Tauberian theorem of [137]. First, all the extra summa-
bility and summability assumptions in [137] are removed from the picture. Second, the proof
of Theorem 1.7 involves showing that Condition (Z) and Condition (H) implies a weaker version
of the Tauberian condition of [137] and showing that this weaker version implies Condition (W)
(see Chapter 3). Therefore, we see that we get the Weyl law (1.1) under a weaker form of the
Tauberian condition of [137].
Condition (Z) and Condition (H) are the types of Tauberian conditions sought for by Alain
Connes in his comments mentioned above.
In practice, they are easier to check than the Tauberian condition of [137]. As we shall see in
the second half of this monograph, they allow us to deal with a wealth of new examples.
Corollary 1.8. The main results of this monograph Proposition 1.1 through Theorem 1.6
hold true if we replace Condition (W) by Condition (H) or Condition (Z).
1.7. Functional analysis – main technical lemmas. The main results mentioned above
follow are consequences of Proposition 1.1 and Theorem 1.7. Therefore, the bulk of our approach
is proving these two results. This involves a significant amount of functional analysis and spectral
theory. More specifically, the proofs of both results rely on three main technical lemmas on weak
Schatten properties of commutators and differences of powers of operators naturally associated
with spectral triples.
In what follows, given any r > 0, we denote by ∥· ∥r,∞the quasi-norm of the weak Schatten
class Lr,∞.
The first new technical result is about the weak Schatten properties of fractional commutators
[|D|−q, a], a ∈A .

established by means of pseudodifferential techniques in the cases where suitable pseudodifferential
calculi are available (see, e.g., [134]). However, it holds in far greater generality and requires
very little regularity for the elements of A . For instance, for Dirac spectral triples over closed
Riemannian spin manifolds, Lemma 1.9 holds for all Lipschitz functions. In addition, the type of
estimate provided by (1.14) is usually not reachable by pseudodifferential techniques.
The other two new technical results provide refinements for spectral triples of deep results of
Connes-Sukochev-Zanin [61, Lemma 5.3] and Sukochev-Zanin [193, Theorem 5.4.2].

In the setup of (unital) spectral triples, Lemma 1.11 is a refinement of [193, Theorem 5.4.2].
The latter result is proved for p > 2 under some strong regularity assumptions (their result
actually holds for non-unital spectral triples).
A weaker version for (unital) Lipschitz regular
spectral triples of summability degree p > 2 is given in [137].
All the above regularity conditions are removed in Lemma 1.11 above. More importantly,
our result does not involve any restriction on the degree of summability, since we always can find
α ∈(0, 1] small enough so that min(1, p −α) > 0.
As in [137] the approach relies on the integral representation given in [61, 193] for differences
(A1/2BA1/2)s−BsAs, ℜs > 1, where A and B are arbitrary positive operators. However, we make
a few observations that simplify matters significantly and lead to stronger results. In particular,
degree summability issues disappear and we do not need to appeal to the difficult and deep results
of [106, 160] that were used in [137]. Thus, even if we get stronger results, with respect to the
approaches of [61, 137, 193], our approach remains fairly elementary.
In addition, it is instrumental for our approaches to the proofs of the key results Proposition 1.1
and Theorem 1.7 to establish the above results for the fractional powers |D|α and the commutators

Lemma 1.12 (Factorization Lemma). Let α ∈[0, 1] and β, γ ∈[0, 1] be such that, either
β + γ < α + 1, or β + γ = α + 1 with max(β, γ) < 1.

The operator Φm
β,γ in (1.16) is defined in terms of double operator-integrals (DOIs) in the
sense of Birman-Solomyak [20, 23, 33] (see Eq. (5.21)). Factorization results for commutators
and differences of functions of positive operators in terms of DOIs are not new (see, e.g., [31,
32, 85, 124, 126, 159]). The proof of our factorization result is actually a mere adaptation
to our setup of the approach of DOI representation of differences of fractional powers of positive
operators in [31]. However, the type of factorization provided by (1.16) in such a wide setting
does not seem to have appeared elsewhere.
Lemma 1.9 is a consequence of the above factorization lemma and some standard induction
argument (see Chapter 3).
As mentioned above, the proofs of Lemma 1.10 and Lemma 1.11 rely on the integral represen-
tations in [61, 193] for (A1/2BA1/2)s −BsAs, ℜs > 1. In the case A = a ∈A++ and B = |D|−α,
α ∈(0, 1] the integrands in this formula involve commutators [|D|−α, az], z ∈C. By Lemma 1.9
these commutators lies in the weak Schatten class L(|α|+1)−1p,∞. Weak Schatten classes Lr,∞
are Banach spaces for r > 1, but for r ≤1 they are only quasi-Banach spaces, and hence are not
locally convex spaces. This results in serious technical difficulties to deal with integration and
analyticity of maps with values in Lr,∞, r ≥1. This is in a nutshell the main technical hurdle
that prevent us to extend to summability degree p ≤2 the approaches of [61, 137, 193].
Our main idea is to use Lemma 1.12 to “factorize out” powers of |D|−1 and get new inte-
grands depending on the bounded commutators [D, az]. Thereby, we trade integrals with inte-
grands in weak Schatten classes for integrals whose integrands are compact operators, for which
standard Bochner integration techniques apply. The Schatten properties stated by Lemma 1.10
and Lemma 1.11 then become consequences of H¨older’s inequality for weak Schatten classes. In
particular, degree summability issues become irrelevant. We refer to Chapter 6 for the full proofs
of Lemma 1.10 and Lemma 1.11.

## Page 12


The main new idea in the proof of Proposition 1.1 is using Lemma 1.10, along with Lemma 1.9
and the perturbation theory of Birman-Solomyak [21, 29]), to show in substance that if the
spectral asymptotics (1.18) hold for some q0 > 0, then they hold for all q > 0. Note that for
achieving this it is crucial to the allow exponent of |D|−α in Lemma 1.10 to range over all (0, 1]
instead of restricting ourselves to α = 1 (compare [137]).
As it turns out, Condition (W) is actually equivalent to the spectral asymptotics (1.18) for
q = 2 (see Lemma 3.8). This allows us to complete the proof of Proposition 1.1 (see Chapter 3
for the full details).
1.9. Sketch of proof of Theorem 1.7. The proof of Theorem 1.7 clarifies the relation-
ships between the conditions of this monograph and the Tauberian condition of [137] (see also
Condition 4.2 for the statement of that condition). In [137] the condition is required to hold
for all a ∈A +. This is arguably too strong a requirement. We introduce a weaker condition
(Condition (Z0); see Chapter 4). This condition is required to hold for all a ∈A++ only.
For one thing, we use Lemma 1.11, along with the well-known Ikehara’s Tauberian theorem,
to show that Condition (Z0) implies Condition (W). The role of Lemma 1.11 in this proof is
paramount. For the other thing, we show that Condition (Z) and Condition (H) of Theorem 1.7
both imply Condition (Z0), and hence they imply Condition (W). This establishes Theorem 1.7.
We refer to Chapter 4 for the full proof of Theorem 1.7.
2. Bounded Euclidean Domains
In Chapter 7, we explain how the main results of this monograph allows us to recover well
known semiclassical Weyl laws for fractional Schr¨odinger operators associated with boundary value
problems on Euclidean domains with smooth boundary. We also obtain an integration formula in
this setting, which seems to be a new result.
Let Ωbe a bounded domain in Rn, n ≥2, with smooth boundary ∂Ω. We also let ∆Ωbe
either the Dirichlet Laplacian or the Neumann Laplacian on Ω. In either case we get an essentially
selfadjoint strongly elliptic 2nd order differential operator whose spectrum is non-negative and
purely discrete. To get a spectral triple we consider the ∗-algebra,
˙C∞(Ω) := C1 + C∞
c (Ω).
That is, ˙C∞(Ω) is the algebra of C∞-functions on Ωthat are constant near the boundary ∂Ω. This
is a ∗-subalgebra of the C∗-algebra of bounded continuous functions on Ω, and so it is represented
in L2(Ω) by bounded multiplication operators. Its norm closure is the C∗-algebra,
˙C(Ω) := C + C0(Ω),
where C0(Ω) is the (closed) ideal of continuous functions on Ωthat converges to 0 near the
boundary ∂Ω. In other words, ˙C(Ω) is the space of continuous functions on Ωthat are constant
on the boundary ∂Ω.

3. Closed Riemannian Manifolds
Chapter 8 has two main parts. In the first part, we explain how the main results of this
monograph enables us to recover well known semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas for
closed manifolds, as well as Weyl laws for Steklov eigenvalue problems on compact manifolds with
boundary.
In the second part, we explain that in the case of spectral triples associated with algebras of
zeroth order ΨDOs, Proposition 1.1 provides us with a new proof of the Weyl laws of Birman-
Solmyak for negative order ΨDOs. In fact, we shall obtain a more general result for suitable
closures the spaces of (negative order) ΨDOs. This ulitmately leads to a far-reaching refinement
of Connes’ trace theorem [52] and of its version in [113].
In what follows, (M n, g) is a closed Riemannian manifold, and E is a Hermitian vector bundle
over M.

3.2. Steklov eigenvalues. The example of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator is of spe-
cial interest because it is closely related to Steklov eigenvalue problems. Assume that M is the
boundary of a compact manifold (X
n+1, g) with interior X such that g|T M = g on M. Given
any real-valued function γ(x) in Ln(M) (n ≥2) or in LlogL(M) (n = 1), the weighted Steklov
problem associated with γ(x) is the eigenvalue problem,
 ∆gu = 0
on X,

on M,
where ∂ν is the outward normal derivative. (The ordinary Steklov problem correspond to the
constant weight γ = 1.)
As is well known, the positive (resp., negative) Steklov eigenvalues agree with the positive
(resp., negative) eigenvalue of the Birman-Schwinger operator Λ−1/2
g

Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator (see also Chapter 8). As a result, the spectral asymptotics (1.23)
for DE = Λg and q = 1 are equivalent to Weyl laws for the positive/negative Steklov eigenvalues.
This allows us to recover results of Agranovich [2] and Suslina [194] (n ≥1) and Rozenblum [165]
(n = 1) (see Proposition 8.10 for the precise statement).
Note also that Weyl laws for Steklov eigenvalues associated the weights in Ln(M) (n ≥2) or
in LlogL(M) (n = 1) imply Weyl law for ordinary Steklov eigenvalues on surfaces with Lipschitz
boundaries (see [109, 166]).
In addition, the integration formula (1.25) for DE = Λg enables us to reformulate the Steklov
Weyl laws in terms of the NC integral (see Proposition 8.12).

particular, we obtain spectral measurability, whereas the focus in [113] is on strong measurability.
The spectral asymptotics (1.28)–(1.29) and the integration formulas (1.30)–(1.31) have equiv-
alent reformulations in terms of suitable closures of the spaces Ψm(M, E), m < 0.
For s ≥0, we let H (s) be the Sobolev space with Bessel potential |DE|s = (1+(∇E)∗∇E)s/2.
We define H (−s) to be its antilinear dual. If m < 0, then every P ∈Ψm(M, E) uniquely extends
to a continuous linear operator P : H (m/2) →H (|m|/2). We define Ψm(M, E) to be the closure of
Ψm(M, E) in L (H (m/2), H (|m|/2)). The adjoint map P →P ∗uniquely extends to an isometric
antilinear involution on Ψm(M, E). In addition, the principal symbol map on Ψm(M, E) uniquely
extends to a continuous linear map σm : Ψm(M, E) →C(S∗M, π∗End(E)) (see Chapter 8).
Let Φ : L (H (m/2), H (|m|/2)) →L (L2(M, E)) be the Banach space isomorphism,
Φ(T) = |DE||m|/2T|DE||m|/2,
T ∈L (H (m/2), H (|m|/2)).
This induces an a Banach space isomorphism Φ : Ψm(M, E) →Ψ0(M, E). This isomorphism then
provides a one-to-one correspondence between for operators in Ψ0(M, E) and equivalent results
for operators in Ψm(M, E).
Under this correspondence, the spectral asymptotics (1.28)–(1.29) are equivalent to the fol-
lowing extension of the Weyl law for negative order ΨDOs of Birman-Solomyak [26, 27, 28].

4. Open Manifolds with Conformally Cusp Metrics
In Chapter 9, we apply the main results of this monograph to get semiclassical Weyl laws and
integration formulas for some class of open Riemannian manifolds. In particular, the semiclassical
Weyl laws that we obtain complement recent semiclassical Weyl laws for complete Riemanian
manifolds by Braverman [36] and Dai-Yan [65] (see Remark 9.3).
We work in the following geometric setup. We let Xn be an open manifold, which is the interior
of a compact manifold X with closed boundary M n−1 = ∂X and boundary defining function x.
We assume that X is endowed with a Riemannian metric of the form,
(1.36)
g = x2rg0,
r > 1
n,
where g0 is a cusp metric on X (see Chapter 9 for the precise definition). We also denote by h0
the Riemannian metric induced by g0 on the boundary M = ∂X. The condition r > 1/n ensures
that (X, g) has finite volume. Moreover, (X, g) is a complete Riemannian manifold if and only if
r ≤1.
Examples of manifolds with conformally cusp metrics include hyperbolic metrics of finite
volume (in which case r = 1; see [150]). Another class of examples is provided by metric horns in
the sense of Cheeger [42] (see also [120]) for which r > 1.
4.1. Dirac Operators. Assume further that X is spin. We denote by /Dg the Dirac operator
of X acting on the sections of the spinor bundle /SX. In addition, we denote by /Dh0 the Dirac
operator of M = ∂X with respect to the metric (1.36). We make the following assumptions:
• g0 is an exact cusp metric in the sense of Melrose [141] (the definition is recalled in
Chapter 9).
• ker /Dh0 = {0}, i.e., there are no (non-zero) harmonic spinors on M.

5. Quantum Tori
In Chapter 10, we apply the main results of this chapter to quantum tori to get new semiclassi-
cal Weyl laws and integration formulas. We look at spectral triples associated with the square-root
Laplacian and the Dirac operator. For the square root Laplacian the semiclassical Weyl laws were
conjectured by Edward McDonald and the author in [132]. The integration formulas that we
obtain are refinements of previous integration formulas [132, 136, 155].

6. Sub-Riemannian Geometry
In Chapter 11, we apply the main results of the monograph to get semiclassical Weyl laws
and integration formulas in sub-Riemannian geometry. These results connect nicely with a string
of recent results on spectral asymptotics and quantum limits in sub-Riemannian geometry (see,
e.g., [11, 48, 50, 75, 76, 77, 78, 121, 138]). In addition, in the contact case we obtain stronger
forms of the integration formulas of [114, 153].
In what follows, we let (M, H, gH) be a closed sub-Riemannian manifold, where M is an n-
dimensional closed manifold, H is a Lie-bracket generating subbundle of the tangent bundle TM
of rank m and gH is a sub-Riemannian metric on H, i.e., a smooth field of positive-definite inner
products on its fibers. The Lie-bracket generating condition H then ensures that, at every point
x0 ∈M, we have a filtration,
(1.47)
H(x0) =: H1(x0) ⊆H2(x0) ⊆· · · ⊆Hr(x0) = TM(x0),
where Hw(x0), w ≥2, is the subspace spanned by iterated Lie brackets,
[Xi1, [Xi2, · · · [Xik−1, Xik] · · · ]](x0),
k ≤w,
with Xi1, . . . , Xik ranging over all H-valued vector fields near x0.
Sub-Riemannian structures and Lie-bracket generating distributions naturally occur in a va-
riety of settings. This includes control theory of non-holonomic systems of vector fields as those
that comes into play in computer vision and image processing, Asian option in finance, dynamics
of polymer, and distributions of clusters in space in astronomy (see [35]). They also appear on
(graded) nilpotent Lie groups and their quotients by lattices (i.e., nilmanifolds).
In addition, Sub-Riemannian structures appear in the setting of parabolic geometry [38],
including boundary-geometry of symmetric spaces of rank ≥2. Contact manifolds (e.g., cosphere
17


## Page 22


bundles of positive line bundles) and their various generalizations yield additional examples. A
related class of examples is provided by boundaries of complex domains (e.g., odd-dimensional
Euclidean spheres), and more generally finite-type Cauchy-Riemann manifolds.
We further assume that M is equipped with a smooth measure ν; it allows us to define an inner
product on L2(M). In this setting, the analogue of the Laplacian is provided by the sub-Laplacian
∆H : C∞
c (M) →C∞
c (M). We refer to Chapter 11 for its precise definition. This is a formally
selfadjoint 2nd order differential operator. In the Riemannian setting, i.e., H = TM, in which
case gH is a Riemannian metric, we recover the Laplacian associated with ν and this Riemannian
metric. In general, if X1, . . . , Xm is a local orthonormal frame of H, then, locally, we have
∆H =

1≤j≤m
 −X2



where divν is the divergence of vector fields with respect to the measure ν.
In particular, if
m < n, then ∆H is not elliptic. However, by a celebrated result of H¨ormander [104], the Lie-
bracket generating condition ensures that ∆H and the heat operator ∆H + ∂t on M × R are both
hypoelliptic with loss of derivatives.
As the manifold M is closed, the hypoellipticity of ∆H ensures it is essentially selfadjoint and
has a non-negative and disrete spectrum. This spectrum then can be arranged as a non-decreasing
sequence,
0 ≤λ0(∆H) ≤λ1(∆H) ≤λ2(∆H) ≤· · · ,
where each eigenvalue is repeated according to multiplicity.
We shall further assume that the sub-Riemannian structure is equiregular. This means that
in (1.47) the step r can be chosen independently of x0 and the dimensions dim Hw(x0), w ≥2,
do not vary with x0. This condition is satisfied in many examples of sub-Riemannian structures.
For j = 2, . . . , r, the vector spaces Hj(x0), x0 ∈M, can then be organized as smooth subbundles
Hw ⊆TM. We thus get a filtration by subbundles,
{0} = H0 ⊆H1 = H ⊆H2 ⊆· · · ⊆Hr = TM.
This filtration is compatible with the Lie bracket of vector fields, i.e., [Hj, Hk] ⊆Hj+k. Manifolds
equipped with such filtrations of their tangent bundles are called filtered manifolds (see, e.g., [38]).
The sub-Riemannian dimension (or sR-dimension) of M then is defined by
N :=
X
1≤j≤r
j dim (Hj/Hj−1) .
This is also the Hausdorff dimension with respect to the Carath´eodory metric defined by (H, gH)
(see, e.g., [1]).
Under the equiregularity assumption, M´etivier [145] obtained a Weyl law for the sub-Laplacian
∆H. Namely, as j →∞we have

In the step 2 case (i.e., r = 2) the relevant pseudodifferential calculus to study sub-Laplacians
is the Heisenberg calculus of Beals-Greiner [13] and Taylor [197]. A version of this calculus for
arbitrary filtered manifolds was presented by Melin in the unpublished preprint [140]. As shown by

In the step 2 case the integration for-
mula (1.54) is known (see [153]; see also [114] for the contact case).
However, even in that

conjectured that we should have strong measurability and spectral asymptotics of the form (1.50).
Therefore, our results establish these conjectures in the far more general setting of (equiregular)
19


## Page 24


sub-Riemannian geometry. Moreover, our approach bypasses the technical considerations in [114]
inherent to the C∗-approach to the principal symbol that is used there.
We refer to § 6 of Chapter 11 for a comparison of the integration formulas (1.53)–(1.54) with
the version for ΨHDOs of Connes’ trace theorem that recently appeared in [131].
In the contact case, the Weyl density γ(∆H) can be computed explicitly thanks to the results
of [14] (see also [197]). This gives explicit semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas for con-
tact manifolds (see Theorem 11.14 and Theorem 11.15 for the precise statements). In particular,
this provides a refinement of the integration formulas of [114, 153].
The above results actually hold in far greater generality than stated above. First, the results
of [67, 68, 154] actually apply to all positive selfadjoint Rockland operators on general filtered
manifolds (see Remark 11.12). Therefore, our approach remains valid if we replace ∆H by anu
such operator (see Remark 11.12). Second, in the light of the results of [6, 50], it is expected that
our results can be extended to a large class of singular sub-Riemannian manifolds, including the
Baouendi-Grushin example (see the discussion on this in §5 of Chapter 11).
Finally, we refer to §6 of Chapter 11 for a comparison of the integration formulas (1.53)–(1.54)
with the extension of Connes’ trace theorem to ΨHDOs in the recent preprint of McDonald [131]
(which appeared near the end of the completion of this manuscript).
7. Final comments
The focus of this memoir is on unital spectral triples. It would be of upmost importance to
extend the results of this memoir to non-unital spectral triples. This would allow us to include
in our framework Euclidean spaces, infinite volume geometries, and quantum Euclidean spaces.
Condition (W) is really specific to unital spectral triples. However, the equivalent condition (3.7)
and the Tauberian conditions (Z) and (H) continue to make sense in the non-unital case with
minor modifications. In addition, it is clear that the considerations on double operator-integrals
of this memoir hold in much greater generality.
Condition (W) is stated in terms of classical Weyl laws. However, in a number of examples
we actually have nonclassical Weyl laws in the sense of Barry Simon [181]. This means Weyl laws
involving logarithmic of the spectral parameter, instead of sole powers. For instance, if Riemann
Hypothesis holds and we let D be the Dirac operator whose eigenvalues are the imaginary parts of
the zeros of Riemann’s zeta function (see [54, 60]), then by the Riemann-van Managoldt formula
the counting function of D2 satisfies a nonclassical Weyl law of the form,
N
 D2; λ2

as λ →∞.
In fact, in a number examples arising from manifolds with infinite volumes or with singularities
the corresponding geometric operators may satisfy nonclassical Weyl laws.
From the point of view of spectral triples this amounts to replace summability in terms of
weak Schatten classes by summability in terms of weak Lorentz ideals Lg of the form,
(1.55)
Lg := {T; µj(T) = O (g(j))} ,
g(t) = t−p(log(t + 1))q,
p, q > 0.
Therefore, a proper extension of the results of this memoir to non-unital spectral triples should
include spectral triples that are summable with respect to the above types of weak Lorentz ideals.
A careful study of the relationships between Connes’ integration in weak Lorentz ideals and
nonclassical Weyl laws is given in the forthcoming paper [158] (see also [84, 90, 125]). It also
seems likely that, at least for unital spectral triples, the considerations on double operator-integrals
of this memoir hold verbatim if we replace weak Schatten classes by powers of any weak Lorentz
ideal (1.55) with p = 1. We will need to allow poles of higher order in Condition (Z) and incorporate
logarithmic terms in the heat trace expansion in Condition (H).
Finally, the main results of this memoir for a given spectral triple (A , H , D) involves fractional
powers of |D|, rather than of D, or, equivalently fractional powers of D2. As pointed out to the
author by Magnus Goffeng [88], it would be interesting to extend the results of this memoir to
generalized spectral triples like those considered by Goffeng-Mesland [89, Appendix A], or for a
variant of the definition there. For instance, we could consider triples (A , H , D) that are like
20


## Page 25


spectral triples, for which the condition on the boundedness of the commutators [D, a], a ∈A , is
replaced by the boundedness of the operators (1+D2)(1−m)/4m[D, a](1+D2)(1−m)/4m, a ∈A , for
some number m > 0. For instance, if (M n, g) is a closed Riemannian manifold and A = C∞(M),
the above condition is satisfied by D = ∆g for m = 2. This would bypass some of the technical
difficulties to checking in some examples that fractional powers of natural operators give rise to
(ordinary) spectral triples.
8. Organization of the paper
The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Chapter 2, we review the main background
on weak Schatten ideals, Connes’ integration and their relationships with Weyl laws and the
Birman-Schwinger principle. In Chapter 3, we prove Proposition 1.1 (assuming Lemma 1.10 and
Lemma 1.12) and we deduce from it Theorem 1.2 through Theorem 1.6. In Chapter 4, we prove
Theorem 1.7 assuming Lemma 1.10. In Chapter 5, we use double integral operators techniques to
prove Lemma 1.12. In Chapter 6, we prove Lemma 1.10 and Lemma 1.11.
In the remaining chapters we apply the main results of the monograph to various settings.
In Chapter 7, we look at the semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas for powers of the
Dirichlet and Neumann Laplacians on bounded Euclidean domains. In Chapter 8, we deal with
the results for closed Riemanian manifolds. Chapter 9 is devoted to proving semiclassical Weyl
laws and integration formulas for open manifolds with conformally cusp metrics. In Chapter 10
the focus is on quantum tori. In particular, we prove Conjecture 1.14. In Chapter 11, we prove
semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas in sub-Riemannian geometry.

2. Connes’ integration
One of the main goals of noncommutative geometry [53] is to translate into the Hilbert space
formalism of quantum mechanics the main tools of differential geometry. In this framework the
notion of integral corresponds to positive traces on the weak trace class L1,∞. Such traces are
annihilated by finite rank operators (see, e.g., [129]) and are always continuous (see, e.g., [155,
Remark 2.3]), and so they are annihilated by (L1,∞)0.
There is a whole zoo of positive traces on L1,∞(see, e.g., [129, 130] and the references
therein).
One important class of such traces is provided by Dixmier traces Trω : L1,∞→C
(see [69]; see also [53, 129, 157]). We then say that an operator A ∈L1,∞is measurable if the
value Trω(A) does not depend on of the choice of the Dixmier trace. Equivalently (see [129, 157]),
the operator A is measurable if and only if it satisfies the following Tauberian condition,

4. Connes’ integration and Birman-Schwinger principle
Let H0 be an unbounded selfadjoint operator with non-negative spectrum. We assume that 0
is not in the essential spectrum of H0, i.e., it is either not in Sp(H0) or is an isolated eigenvalue
with finite multiplicity. These conditions are automatically satisfied if H0 has compact resolvent.

as a form-sum (see, e.g., [183]). This operator is selfadjoint, bounded from below, and has same
essential spectrum as H0. This implies that the negative part of its spectrum consists of finitely
isolated eigenvalues. Moreover, if H0 has a compact resolvent, then HV has a compact resolvent
as well (loc. cit.).

CHAPTER 3
Spectral asymptotics, Semiclassical Weyl laws, and
Integration Formulas
In this chapter, after presenting Condition (W) and some of its implications, we prove Lemma 1.9
and Proposition 1.1, assuming Lemma 1.10 and Lemma 1.12 whose proofs are postponed to Chap-
ter 5 and Chapter 6 , respectively. We shall then prove the main semiclassical Weyl laws and
integration formulas stated in Introduction (i.e., Theorem 1.2 through Theorem 1.6) as conse-
quences of Proposition 1.1.
1. Spectral triples
In the framework of noncommutative geometry, noncommutative manifolds are represented
by spectral triples. We shall use the following definition of spectral triples.
Definition 3.1. A spectral triple is a triple (A , H , D), where A is a unital ∗-algebra repre-
sented by bounded operators on the (separable) Hilbert space H and D is a selfadjoint unbounded
operator on H with compact resolvent such that
a (dom(D)) ⊆dom(D)
and
[D, a] ∈L (H )
∀a ∈A .
We further say that (A , H , D) is p-summable, with p > 0, if the partial inverse D−1 is in Lp,∞.
The prototype of a spectral triple is given by the Dirac spectral triple (C∞(M), L2(M, /S), /D),
where M is a closed Riemannian spin manifold, L2(M, /S) is the Hilbert space of L2-spinors on M
and /D is the Dirac operator of M acting on spinors. Another example is the square-root Laplacian
spectral triple (C∞(M), L2(M),
p
∆g), where ∆g is the (positive) Laplacian of (M, g) acting on
functions. Both spectral triples are p-summable with p = dim M.
Some authors also require some regularity properties for the spectral triple. A spectral triple
(A , H , D) is called Lipschitz regular if

Throughout the rest of this chapter, we let (A , H , D) be a p-summable spectral triple with
p > 0. We denote by A the closure of A in L (H ), and let A + be its cone of positive elements
(i.e., selfadjoint elements with non-negative spectrum). We also denote by A ++ the open cone of
its invertible elements (i.e., selfadjoint elements with positive spectrum). The cone A++ is then
defined be to A ∩A ++, i.e., it consists of elements of A that are positive and invertible in A .

Remark 3.3. The assumption on the linearity of τ is not necessary. In the main examples
the linearity is immediate. Moreover, the map τ will be later identified with a linear map (see
Remark 3.21). Therefore, there is no harm in assuming it beforehand.
Remark 3.4. The fact that τ is a non-zero positive linear map on A ensures that τ is
continuous and τ(a) > 0 for all a ∈A ++.
Remark 3.5. If A = A∗∈L (H ) and we set DA = D+A, then (A , H , DA) is a p-summable
spectral triple. It can also be shown that if (A , H , DA) satisfies Condition (W), then (A , H , DA)
still satisfies this condition with the same map τ (see Lemma 3.10 below). Thus, Condition (W)
is invariant under bounded perturbations D →D + A. This includes the inner fluctuations of the
metric in the sense of Connes (see, e.g., [55]).

7. Extension to Unbounded Potentials – Proofs of Theorem 1.5 and Theorem 1.6
In this section, we shall prove Proposition 1.4 and use it to obtain Theorem 1.5 and Theo-
rem 1.6. These results provide a general paradigm for extending to suitable classes of unbounded
potentials the spectral asymptotics of Proposition 1.1 and the semiclassical Weyl law and integra-
tion formulas provided by Theorem 1.2 and Theorem 1.3.
The approach of this section is a mere elaboration of the well-known approach of Birman-
Solomyak (see, e.g., [24]) and Simon [180] to semiclassical Weyl laws for Lp-potentials. The only
technical difference is the fact that, due to the noncommutativity of our general setup, we need
to work with noncommutative Lp-spaces.
7.1. Cwikel-type estimates for spectral triples. As mentioned in the Introduction (Chap-
ter 1), on Rn or bounded domains in Rn, the extensions to Lr-potentials can be obtained by comb-
ing the perturbation theory of Birman-Solomyak (see Proposition 2.4) and with deep estimates
of Cwikel [64] and its generalizations (see, e.g., [124, 165, 168, 186, 191] and the references
therein). In the setup of spectral triples we can formulate Cwikel-type estimates for spectral triples
as follows.
For s > 0 denote by H (s) the Sobolev space with Bessel potential (1+D2)s/2, i.e., the Hilbert
space consisting of dom(1+D2)s/2 equipped with the Hilbert norm ξ →∥(1+D2)s/2ξ∥. We denote
by H (−s) its antilinear dual, i.e., the Hilbert space of continuous antilinear forms on H (s). Any
operator V ∈L (H (s), H (−s)) defines a quadratic form on H (s) by
QV (ξ, η) = ⟨V ξ, η⟩,
ξ, η ∈H (s),
where ⟨·, ·⟩: H (−s) × H (s) →C is the duality pairing. The adjoint V ∗∈L (H (s), H (−s)) is
given by
⟨V ∗ξ, η⟩= ⟨V η, ξ⟩,
ξ, η ∈H (s).
In particular, QV ∗= QV , and so QV is symmetric if and only if V ∗= V . Note also that any
T ∈L (H ) induces an operator T : H (s) →H (s) such that
⟨Tξ, η⟩= ⟨Tξ|η⟩
∀ξ, η ∈H .
This yields a continuous ∗-embedding of L (H ) into L (H (s), H (−s)), and so this induces a
continuous ∗-embedding of A into L (H (s), H (−s)).
Given any q > 0, the Hilbert space H (q) is contained in the domain of the quadratic form of
(D2)q. In addition, |D|−q maps continuously H to H (q). It also extends to a bounded operator
|D|−q : H (−q) →H given by

|D|−qξ|η

=

ξ, |D|−qη

,
ξ ∈H (−q), η ∈H .
Thus, if V ∈L (H (q), H (−q)), then the operator |D|−qV |D|−q is bounded on H . This operator
is selfdajoint (resp., compact) if V is selfadjoint (resp., compact).
It follows from this that if V = V ∗∈L (H (q), H (−q)) is compact, then the symmetric
quadratic form QV is (D2)q-form compact. This allows us to define the operator H(q)
V
:= (D2)q+V
as a form sum, i.e., as the selfadjoint operator whose quadratic form is Q(D2)q +QV . This operator
36


## Page 41


is bounded from below and has compact resolvent, and hence it has pure discrete spectrum (see,
e.g., [183]).

with respect to the weak operator topology. As τ is finite, for any r ∈(0, ∞) we may define the
noncommutative Lr-space Lr(M )1 as the closure of M with respect to the quasi-norm,

Remark 3.24. In (i) we use the space Lˆr(M ) instead of Lr(M ) to ensure the continuity of the

Remark 3.25. We refer to [124] for extensions of the Cwikel estimates to a large class of

tori in [132, 133] (see also Chapter 10). It would be interesting to use the framework of [124] to
get further examples of spectral triples satisfying Condition (Cr).
7.2. Spectral asymptotics. If Condition (Cr) holds, then we denote by Vr the Banach
space completion of A with respect to the norm ∥·∥(r). We have a continuous inclusion of A into
Vr, as well as a continuous inclusion of Vr into Lˆr(M ) thanks to (i). Moreover, the involution
a →a∗of A uniquely extend to an isometric antilinear involution of Vr.

CHAPTER 4
Tauberian Conditions – Proof of Theorem 1.7
This chapter is devoted to proving Theorem 1.7, which provides Tauberian conditions that
give Condition (W). As a byproduct of the approach of this chapter, we will even get a stronger
version for spectral triples of the Tauberian theorem of [137] without any extra regularity or
summability conditions (see Remark 4.5).
Throughout this chapter we let (A , H , D) be a p-summable spectral triple, with p > 0
arbitrary. We keep on using the notation of the previous chapters.

2. Condition (Z0)
In our setting, the Tauberian condition of [137] is formulated as follows.
Condition 4.2 (Tauberian Condition of [137]). For every a ∈A +, the function
Tr


It is a too strong a requirement to have the condition to hold for all positive elements of the C∗-
algebra A . In fact, in examples where A is an algebra of ΨDOs it may be technically cumbersome
to check Condition 4.2 for all elements of A +, whereas it is comparatively straightforward to check
Condition (W).
In addition, as mentioned in Introduction, it was asked by Alain Connes to relate Condition 4.2
to more standard Tauberian conditions involving the zeta functions Tr[a|D|−s], a ∈A , instead
of the functions Tr[as|D|−s], a ∈A +. He also pointed out that closeness under holomorphic
functional calculus could come into play.
In fact, what is relevant in our setting is a weakening of Condition 4.2 provided by Condi-
tion (Z0) below. For one thing, that condition implies Condition (W) (see Lemma 4.4 below).
For the other things, it will be shown that Condition (Z0) is implied by both Condition (Z) and
Condition (H) (see Lemma 4.10 and Lemma 4.14 below). As a result, this will show Condition (Z)
and Condition (H) both imply Condition (W), thereby proving Theorem 1.7.

3. Condition (Z)
We may replace Condition (Z0) by a more standard Tauberian condition, which is easier to
check in practice. Incidentally, Condition (Z) is the type of Tauberian condition sought for by
Alain Connes.
In what follows by a Fr´echet subalgebra of A we shall mean a subalgebra B which is endowed
with a Fr´echet algebra topology which is stronger than the topology of A . Recall that if B is a
unital Fr´echet algebra, then the inverse map x →x−1 is continuous on its set of invertible elements
B−1 (see [152, 203]). Therefore, if B is a Fr´echet subalgebra of A containing the unit, then it
is holomorphically closed (i.e., it is closed under holomorphic functional calculus) if and only if it
is inverse closed (i.e., B−1 = A
−1 ∩B).
Considering Fr´echet subalgebras ensures that Banach-Steinhaus theorem holds. The need for
holomorphic closedness stems from the following lemma.

4. Condition (H)
There is a closed relationship between singularities of zeta functions and short-time behaviours
of traces of heat semigroups (see, e.g., [97]). In various examples the latter are often simpler to
study. Therefore, it stands for reason to seek for a heat semigroup version of Condition (Z).

CHAPTER 5
Double Integral Operators – Proof of Lemma 1.12
This chapter is devoted to proving Lemma 1.12. This lemma provides factorization formulas
for fractional commutators. These formulas play crucial roles in the proof of Lemma 1.9 in Chap-
ter 3, and in the proofs of Lemma 1.10 and Lemma 1.11 in Chapter 6. Those lemmas are the key
ingredients in the proofs of the main results of this monograph (see Chapter 3 and Chapter 4).
The proof of Lemma 1.12 relies on results on basic results for double operator-integrals (DOIs).
These results were established by Birman and Solomyak in the 60s and 70s.

These operators (when they make sense) are special instances of double operator integrals (DOIs)

Remark 5.10. Lemma 1.12 is stated in much more generality than what is needed for this
monograph. In fact, for our purpose we only need the result in the following cases:
(i) 0 < α ≤1 and β = γ = 0.
(ii) 0 < α ≤1, γ = |m| and 0 < β < 1.
The first case is used in the proof of Lemma 1.9.
The second case is needed in the proof of
Lemma 1.10, for which it is absolutely crucial to be able to take γ = α, including for α = 1 (see
Eqs. (6.10) and (6.12) and Remark 6.7 on this point).

CHAPTER 6
Proofs of Lemma 1.10 and Lemma 1.11
This chapter is devoted to proving Lemma 1.10 and Lemma 1.11, which are two the main key
technical lemmas used the proof of Proposition 1.1 in Chapter 3 and in the proof of Theorem 1.7
in Chapter 4. Those results lead to the main results of this monograph.
Throughout this chapter we let (A , H , D) be a p-summable spectral with p > 0 arbitrary.
We shall keep on using the notation of the previous chapters.
1. Preliminary lemmas
In this section, we prove several lemmas that will be used in the proofs of Lemma 1.10 and
Lemma 1.11.
The following lemma is a version of Lemma 4.1 for imaginary powers.
Lemma 6.1. If X is a (non-zero) positive compact operator on H , then Xit, t ∈R, is a
bounded strongly continuous family in L (H ).
Proof. As with Lemma 4.1, the above result is standard. We include a proof for the reader’s
convenience. Let (ξj)j≥0 be an orthonormal eigenfamily of X such that Xξj = λj(X)ξj for all
j ≥0, where λ0(X) ≥λ1(X) ≥· · · are the eigenvalues of X. The family Xit, t ∈R, then is a
strongly continuous family in L (H ), since, for every ξ ∈H , we have
Xitξ =
X
λj(X)>0
λj(X)it ⟨ξj|ξ⟩ξj,
t ∈R,
where the series converges in H uniformly on compact sets of R. Note also that ∥Xit∥= 1, since
|λj(X)it| = 1 if λj(X) > 0 (such an eigenvalue exists, since X ̸= 0). In particular, the family Xit,
t ∈R, is bounded in L (H ). The result is proved.
□

## Page 69


CHAPTER 7
Boundary Value Problems on Bounded Euclidean Domains
In this chapter, we explain how the main results of this monograph enable us to recover well
known spectral asymptotics and semiclassical Weyl laws for Laplacians on bounded domains in
Euclidean space. Incidentally, we shall also obtain integrations, which seem to be new in this
setting.
The semiclassical Weyl laws mentioned above were essentially established in the 60s and 70s.
The only novelty here (if any) is that the spectral theoretic considerations of this monograph
enables us to recover these results from well-known results of Carleman [41] and Minakshisun-
daram [146] that appeared the 30s and 40s.
Throughout this chapter we let Ωbe a bounded domain in Rn, n ≥2, with smooth boundary
∂Ω. We also let ∆Ωbe either the Dirichlet Laplacian or Neumann Laplacian on Ω. We get an
essentially selfadjoint strongly elliptic 2nd order differential operator with non-negative and purely
discrete spectrum.
To get a spectral triple we consider the ∗-algebra,
˙C∞(Ω) := C1 + C∞
c (Ω).
That is, ˙C∞(Ω) is the algebra of C∞-functions on Ωthat are constant near the boundary ∂Ω. This
is a ∗-subalgebra of the C∗-algebra of bounded continuous functions on Ω, and so it is represented
in L2(Ω) by bounded multiplication operators. Its norm closure is the C∗-algebra,
˙C(Ω) := C + C0(Ω),
where C0(Ω) is the (closed) ideal of continuous functions on Ωthat converges to 0 near the
boundary ∂Ω. In other words, ˙C(Ω) is the space of continuous functions on Ωthat are constant
on the boundary ∂Ω.

Remark 7.3. The results of this chapter should hold for more general local boundary condi-
tions. At least for continuous potentials, they should hold on compact manifolds with boundary
conditions as those considered in [95], since in this case the above mentioned results of [95, §4.4–5]
apply. This includes the spectral triple of Levy-Iochum [107], which is using the Dirac operator
under a (local) chiral boundary condition (see also [12] for a generalization for this construction).

## Page 73


CHAPTER 8
Closed Riemannian Manifolds
The goal of this chapter two-fold.
In Section 1, we explain how the main results of this
monograph enable us to recover various known results regarding semiclassical Weyl laws and
integration formulas on closed Riemannian manifolds. We work in a general setup with spectral
triples associated with algebras of sections of endomorphism bundles and 1st order ΨDOs whose
squares are “generalized Laplacians”. In particular, in the example of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann
operator this allows us to recover Weyl laws for Steklov eigenvalue problems (see Section 2).
These results are not new. As in the previous chapter, the main aim is to show that these
well-known results can be recovered by combining the main results of this monograph with old
results of Minakshisundaram-Pleijel [147], which predate the aforementioned results.
In Section 3, we shall show that by using algebras of zeroth order ΨDOs we still get spectral
triples satisfying Condition (W). This leads to a new proof of the celebrated Weyl laws for negative
order ΨDOs of Birman-Solomyak [26, 27, 28]. In fact, we will obtain these results for suitable
completions of the spaces of ΨDOs. This implies a far-reaching extension of Connes’ trace theorem
to this class of operators. In the scalar case this turns to be equivalent to a stronger form for the
integration formula of Kordyukov-Sukochev-Zanin [113]. The C∗-extension of compact operators
provided by the principal symbol map also plays a crucial role in this approach.
Finally, for sake of completeness, we explain in an appendix how an elaboration of the original
arguments of Connes [52] enables us to get weaker version of our integration formula. In the scalar
case this is actually enough to recover the integration formula of [113].
Throughout this chapter, we let (M n, g), n ≥1, be a closed Riemannian manifold, and let E
be a Hermitian vector bundle over M.
1. Semiclassical Weyl laws and integration formulas
Let DE : C∞(M, E) →C∞(M, E) be a selfadjoint first order elliptic ΨDO such that (DE)2
has same principal symbol as the Laplacian, i.e.,

3. Birman-Solomyak’s Weyl laws and Connes’ trace theorem
We shall now explain how the main results of this monograph, and more precisely Proposi-
tion 1.1, enable us to get a new proof of the Weyl laws of Birman-Solomyak for negative order
pseudodifferential operators. This will lead to a far-reaching refinements of Connes’ trace theo-
rem [52] and its version in [113].

Our approach is partly based on the following observation.
Lemma 8.15. The following triple
 Ψ0(M, E), L2(M, E), DE

is an n-summable spectral triple.
Proof. This result is a mere consequence of the principal symbol of DE being scalar. We
only need to show that if A ∈Ψ0(M, E), then A(dom(DE)) ⊆dom(DE), and the commutator
[DE, A] is bounded. Here dom(DE) = H (1), and so it follows from (8.14) for s = 1 and m = 0
that A(dom(DE)) ⊆dom(DE).
It remains to show that [DE, A] = DEA −ADE is bounded. Here DEA is a first order ΨDO
with principal symbol,
σ1(DEA)(x, ξ) = σ1(DE)(x, ξ)σ0(A)(x, ξ) = |ξ|gσ0(A)(x, ξ).
Likewise, ADE is a first order ΨDO with principal symbol,
σ1(ADE)(x, ξ) = σ0(A)(x, ξ)σ1(DE)(x, ξ) = |ξ|gσ0(A)(x, ξ).
73


## Page 78


In particular, we see that σ1(DEA) = σ1(ADE). Therefore, if we regard the commutator [A, DE] =
ADE −DEA has a ΨDO of order ≤1, then we have σ1([DE, A]) = σ1(DEA)−σ1(ADE) = 0. The
exactness of the sequence (8.9) then ensures that [DE, A] has order ≤0, and hence is bounded.
This completes the proof.
□
In what follows we denote by Ψ0(M, E) the closure of Ψ0(M, E) in L (L2(M, E)). This is a
unital C∗-subalgebra of L (L2(M, E)). Note also that Ψ0(M, E) contains the closed ideal K of
compact operators on L2(M, E), since any such operator is the norm-limit of finite-rank smoothing
operators.
As in [113], another key ingredient of our approach is the following well-known result.
Lemma 8.16. The principal symbol map σ0 : Ψ0(M, E) →C∞(M; π∗End(E)) uniquely ex-
tends to a unital C∗-algebra map,
σ0 : Ψ0(M, E) −→C (M; π∗End(E)) ,
where C (M; π∗End(E)) is the C∗-algebra of continuous sections of π∗End(E). This fits into the
following exact sequence of C∗-algebras,
(8.15)
0 −→K −→Ψ0(M, E)
σ0
−→C (M; π∗End(E)) −→0.
Remark 8.17. The above result is stated by Atiyah-Singer in [7, p. 512]. It follows from
a more general result of H¨ormander [105, Theorem 3.3] (see also [111, Corollary 6.1] and [173,
§11.1]). The result in [105] is stated for scalars ΨDOs on an open of Rn. However, as pointed
out by H¨ormander the result continues to hold for ΨDOs acting on sections of vector bundles over
manifolds (see [105, Remark, p. 159]).
Remark 8.18. Recently, Kordyukov-Sukochev-Zanin [113] constructed a C∗-subalgebra ΠM
of Ψ0(M) that provides a C∗-extension of C(M) by the compact operators of the same form
as (8.15 (see Theorem 1.4 in [113]). It is not shown in [113] that ΠM = Ψ0(M), but this follows
from a straightforward application of the Five Lemma.
Remark 8.19. As Ψ0(M, E) is a unital C∗-subalgebra of L (L2(M, E)), the spectrum in
Ψ0(M, E) of any A ∈Ψ0(M, E) agrees with its spectrum in L (L2(M, E)). We shall denote either
spectrum by Sp(A).

Definition 8.27. Ψm(M, E) is the closure of Ψm(M, E) in L (H (m/2), H (|m|/2)).

4. Appendix
In this appendix, we shall explain that, if we are only interested in showing strong measura-
bility, then the integration formula (8.30) can be obtained by a mere elaboration of the original
arguments of Connes [52] to establish his trace theorem. As a byproduct, we will see that the
integration formula of [113, Theorem 1.5] can be deduced from Connes’s original arguments (see
Remark 8.39 below).
Proof of (8.30) and strong measurability by using Connes’ approach. Recall that
Connes [52] showed that every operator P ∈Ψ−n(M, E) is measurable and satisfies the integration
formula (8.30).

P ∈Ψ−n(M, E) is strongly measurable and satisfies the integration formula (8.30). The proof is
complete.

Remark 8.39. It follows from Remark 8.26 and Remark 8.37 that, in the scalar case, the
integration formula (8.30) yields the integration formula of [113, Theorem 1.5]. Therefore, we
see that [113, Theorem 1.5] can be obtained by an easy elaboration of the original arguments of
Connes [52].

## Page 85


CHAPTER 9
Open Manifolds with Conformally Cusp Metrics
In this chapter, we use the main results of this monograph to get semiclassical Weyl laws and
integration formulas for some open Riemannian manifolds. More precisely, we consider fractional
Schr¨odinger operators associated with Dirac operators and magnetic Laplacians on manifolds with
conformally cusp metrics.
The results of this chapter are new.
The semiclassical Weyl laws that we obtain in this
chapter complement nicely recent semiclassical Weyl laws for complete Riemanian manifolds by
Braverman [36] and Dai-Yan [65] (see Remark 9.3).
1. Conformally Cusp Metrics
Throughout this chapter, we let Xn be an open manifold, which is the interior of a compact
manifold X with closed boundary M n−1 = ∂X. Let x be a boundary defining function, i.e., x is
a non-negative smooth function on X such that x−1(0) = M and dx ̸= 0 on M. We assume that
X is endowed with a Riemannian metric of the form,
g = x2rg0,
r > 1
n,
where g0 is a cusp metric on X, i.e., a (complete) Riemannian metric which in local coordinates
near M takes the form,
g0 = a00(x, y)x−4dx2 +
X
1≤j<n
a0j(x, y)x−2dxdyj +

If in addition a00|M = 1 and a0j|M = 0 for j = 1, . . . , n, then (following Melrose [141]) we say
that g0 is an exact cusp metric. Note that the cusp metric g0 induces on M the metric,
(9.1)

X

hij(0, y)dyidyj.
The condition r > 1/n is exactly the condition that ensures that (X, g) has finite volume. In
addition, (X, g) is a complete Riemannian manifold if and only if r ≤1 ([151]).
Examples of manifolds with conformally cusp metrics with r ≥1/n include hyperbolic metrics
of finite volume (in which case r = 1; see [150]). Another class of examples is provided by metric
horns in the sense of Cheeger [42] (see also [120]) for which r > 1.
2. Dirac operators
Assume further that X is spin. We denote by /Dg the Dirac operator of X acting on the
sections of the spinor bundle /SX. In addition, we denote by /Dh0 the Dirac operator of M with
respect to the metric (9.1). We also make the following assumptions:
• g0 is an exact cusp metric.
• ker /Dh0 = {0}, i.e., there are no (non-zero) harmonic spinors on M.
The second condition holds for instance if (M, h0) has a non-negative scalar curvature which is
non-zero at a point. It also known to hold for generic metrics if M either has dimension n ≥4 or
its fundamental group π1(M) vanishes or is cyclic of odd order (cf. the discussion on this on [150,
p. 3] and the references therein).

3. Magnetic Laplacians
We drop the assumption on X being spin. The ordinary Laplacian of (X, g) need not have
discrete spectrum (see, e.g., [82]). However, as observed by Gol´enia-Moroianu [92] we get a fully
elliptic cusp differential operator with discrete spectrum by coupling the Laplacian with a trapping
magnetic field. Such operators appear in the study of the Aharonov-Bohm effect.
More precisely, let B be a magnetic field of the form B = dA, where the vector potential A is
a cusp real 1-form, i.e., a smooth real 1-form on X which near M is of the form,
(9.4)
A = a0(x, y)x−2dx + ω,
ι∂xω = 0.
where a0(x, y) and ω are smooth up to the boundary. Set A0 = ω|M. Following [92] we shall say
that A is trapping on a given connected component M0 ⊆M if one of the following two conditions
is satisfied:
• a0(0, y) is not constant on M0.
• Either 2πA0 is not closed or it defines a non-integral cohomology class in H(M0, R).
We say that A is trapping on M if it is trapping on every connected component.
The magnetic Laplacian is the differential operator on X defined by
∆A := d∗

CHAPTER 10
Quantum Tori – Proof of Conjecture 1.14
In this Chapter, we apply the main results of this chapter to quantum tori. We focus on the
spectral triples associated with the square-root Laplacian and the Dirac operator. In particular,
we shall prove Conjecture 1.14. The integration formulas that we obtain are refinements of the
integration formulas of [136, 132, 155].

## Page 95


CHAPTER 11
Sub-Riemannian Manifolds
In this Chapter, we apply the main results of this monograph to get semiclassical Weyl laws and
integration formulas for Schr¨odinger operators associated with fractional powers of sub-Laplacians
on sub-Riemannian manifolds. These results are new and connect nicely with a string of recent
results on spectral asymptotics and quantum limits in sub-Riemannian geometry (see, e.g., [11,
48, 50, 75, 76, 77, 78, 121, 138]). In addition, in the contact case we obtain stronger forms of
the integration formulas of [114, 153].
1. Sub-Riemannian manifolds and sub-Laplacians
Throughout this chapter we let M n be a manifold equipped with a sub-Riemannian structure
(Hm, gH). That is, H is a Lie-bracket generating subbundle of the tangent bundle TM of rank m
and gH is a sub-Riemannian metric on H, i.e., a smooth field of positive-definite inner products on
its fibers. The vector fields taking values in H are called horizontal vector fields. The Lie-bracket
generating condition H then ensures that, at every point x0 ∈M, we have a filtration,
(11.1)
H(x0) =: H1(x0) ⊆H2(x0) ⊆· · · ⊆Hr(x0) = TM(x0),
where Hw(x0), w ≥2, is the subspace spanned by iterated Lie brackets,
[Xi1, [Xi2, · · · [Xiw−1, Xiw] · · · ]](x0)
with Xi1, . . . , Xiw ranging over all horizontal vector fields near x0.
Sub-Riemannian structures and Lie-bracket generating distributions naturally occur in a vari-
ety of settings. This includes control theory of non-holonomic systems of vector fields as those that
comes into play in computer vision and image processing, Asian option in finance, dynamics of
polymer, and distributions of clusters in space in astronomy (see [35]). Sub-Riemannian structures
also appear on (graded) nilpotent Lie groups and their quotients by lattices (i.e., nilmanifolds).
To a large extent graded nilpotent groups provide “infinitesimal” models for sub-Riemannian
manifolds.
Sub-Riemannian structures further appear in the setting of parabolic geometry [38], including
boundary-geometry of symmetric spaces of rank ≥2. Contact manifolds (e.g., cosphere bundles
of positive line bundles) and their various generalizations yield additional examples. A related
class of examples is provided by boundaries of complex domains (e.g., odd-dimensional Euclidean
spheres), and more generally finite-type Cauchy-Riemann manifolds. In addition, Engel manifolds
are well known step 3 examples.
We refer to [1, 45, 149] and the various references therein
for more background on sub-Riemannian geometry and more substantial discussions of the above
examples.
In the sub-Riemannian setting the analogue of the Laplacian is the horizontal sub-Laplacian
∆H : C∞
c (M) →C∞
c (M). This operator is constructed as follows. Let ν be a smooth measure
on M. In the equiregular case a natural choice is to take ν to be the Popp measure, since this
measure only depends on the data (H, gH) (see, e.g., [1, 149]). However, we shall not be too
specific about the choice of the measure. The datum of ν defines an inner product,
(11.2)
⟨u|v⟩=
Z
M
u(x)v(x)dν(x),
u, v ∈C∞
c (M).

dHu = (du)|H ,

By duality gH defines a (smooth) field gH∗of positive-definite inner products on the fibers of H∗,
and so we get an inner-product on C∞

⟨ω|η⟩=

The horizontal sub-Laplacian ∆H is defined by

c (M).
That is, ∆H is the operator of the sequilinear form (u, v) →⟨dHu|dHv⟩.
The operator ∆H is a 2nd order differential operator which is formally selfadjoint with respect
to the inner product (11.2). In the Riemannian case, i.e., H = TM and gH = g is a Riemannian
metric, we recover the usual Laplacian associated with the data (g, ν). In general, if X1, . . . , Xm
is a local orthonormal frame of H, then, locally, we have
∆H =

where divν is the divergence of vector fields with respect to the measure ν.
In particular, if
m < n, then ∆H is not elliptic. However, by a celebrated result of H¨ormander [104], the Lie-
bracket generating condition ensures that ∆H and the heat operator ∆H + ∂t on M × R are both
hypoelliptic.
2. Weyl laws and nilpotent approximation
From now on, we assume that M is compact. In this case H¨ormander’s result implies that
∆H is essentially selfadjoint and has compact resolvent. Therefore, its spectrum consists of non-
negative isolated eigenvalues of finite multiplicity. We thus can list its positive eigenvalues as a
non-decreasing sequence λ0(∆H) ≤λ1(∆H) ≤· · · as in (3.3).
To get a Weyl law of the form we further assume that the sub-Riemannian structure is equireg-
ular. This means that in the filtration (11.1) the step r can be chosen independently of x0 and
the dimensions dim Hw(x0), w ≥2, do not vary with x0. Most of the geometric examples of
sub-Riemannian manifolds mentioned above are equiregular sub-Riemannian manifolds.
For w = 2, . . . , r, the vector spaces Hw(x0), x0 ∈M, can then be organized as smooth
subbundles Hw ⊆TM. We thus get a filtration of subbundles,
{0} = H0 ⊆H1 = H ⊆H2 ⊆· · · ⊆Hr = TM.
We make the convention that Hr−1 ⊊TM. The above filtration is further compatible with the
Lie bracket of vector fields, i.e.,
[Hw, Hw′] ⊆Hw+w′
if w + w′ ≤r.
Manifolds equipped with such filtrations are often called filtered manifolds (see, e.g., [38]).

3. Semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas
In the step 2 case the relevant pseudodifferential calculus to study sub-Laplacians is the
Heisenberg calculus of Beals-Greiner [13] and Taylor [197]. A version of this calculus for arbitrary
filtered manifolds was presented by Melin in the unpublished preprint [140]. A polyhomogeneous
version of Melin’s calculus was subsequently constructed by van Erp-Yuncken [199] in terms of
essentially homogeneous distributions on the tangent groupoid of the filtered manifold. In what
follows we shall call ΨHDOs the operators in the pseudodifferential calculis of Melin and van
Erp-Yuncken.
The square root √∆H is a ΨHDO of order 1 (see [67, 68]; see also [154] in the step 2 case). If
f ∈C∞(M), then the model operators at x0 of f√∆H and √∆Hf are both equal to f(x0)
p
∆x0
H .
Thus, the commutator [√∆H, f] has order ≤0 in the ΨHDO calculus, and hence is bounded on
L2
ν(M). It follows that we have a spectral triple,

Remark 11.7. The integration formulas (11.20)–(11.21) are new. In the step 2 case (i.e.,
r = 2) the integration formula (11.20) is known (see [153]; see also [114] for the contact case).
However, even in that case, Theorem 11.6 provides a stronger form of measurability. In fact,
in [114, p. 18] it is conjectured that we should have strong measurability and spectral asymptotics
of the form (11.17). Therefore, our results establish these conjectures in the far more general
setting of (equiregular) sub-Riemannian geometry. Moreover, our approach bypasses the technical
considerations in [114] of the C∗-approach to the principal symbol. Note that it is really un-
clear how this approach can be extended to sub-Riemannian manifolds (or more generally filtered
manifolds) that not locally equivalent to graded nilpotent Lie groups.
Remark 11.8. Unlike in the Riemannian case and Euclidean domain cases (cf. Chapter 7
and Chapter 8), the semiclassical limit (11.19) and the NC integrals in (11.20)–(11.21) are not
expressed in terms of the sub-Riemannian volume (i.e., the Popp measure). This is a consequence
of the Weyl law (11.4), which involves the density γ(∆H)(x). Unless in special cases (e.g., contact
manifolds or nilmanifolds; see below) this density need not be constant. In the terminology of [50]
the measure f →
R
M f(x)γ(∆H)(x)dν(x) is called the Weyl’s measure of (M, H, gH). Therefore,
the integration formula (11.20) asserts that the NC integral recaptures the Weyl’s measure.
Remark 11.9. If E is a Hermitian vector bundle over M and ∇E : C∞(M) →C∞(M, H∗⊗E)
is a horizontal connection, then the above results apply mutatis mutandis to the connection sub-
Laplacian ∆H,E := (∇E)∗∇E. In the above formulas we just need to multiply the spectral density
γ(∆H)(x) by (rk E)γ(∆H)(x), where γ(∆H)(x) is still given by (11.14).
Remark 11.10. In the contact case, the density γ(∆H)(x) can be computed explicitly. This
allows us to explicit semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas for contact manifolds (see
Theorem 11.14 and Theorem 11.15 below).
Remark 11.11 (Nilmanifolds). Let G be a (simply connected) stratified nilpotent Lie group
of step r with Lie algebra g = g1 ⊕· · · ⊕gr (which we identify with T0G). Denote by H the left-
invariant G-subbundle of TG generated by g1, and let g a left-invariant sub-Riemannian metric on
H. This defines a left-invariant sub-Riemannian structure on G of sR dimension N = P i dim gi.
Note that g is uniquely determined by its restriction to g1 and, furthermore, this restriction
uniquely defines an inner product on g which is compatible with its grading (see, e.g., [149]). This
defines a Lebesgue measure on g. Pushing-forward it by the exponential map exp : g →G yields
a Haar measure ν on G, which is precisely its Popp measure in the sense of [149].
If Γ ⊆G is a co-compact lattice, then M := G/Γ is a compact nilmanifold.
The sub-
Riemannian structure (H, g) on G descends to a sub-Riemannian structure on M. Moreover, the
Popp measure ν on G descends to the Popp measure of M. We denote by ∆H the corresponding
sub-Laplacian. As every point of M the model operator of ∆H is the sub-Laplacian ∆G of G. It
follows that, as in the contact case, the local Weyl density γ(∆H)(x) is constant and equal to
γG := Γ

Remark 11.12 (Rockland Operators). The results of [67, 68, 154] actually apply to a large
class of operators beyond the class of sub-Laplacians, namely, positive Rockland differential op-
erators. In particular, they apply to the Laplacians associated with BGG sequences in parabolic
97


## Page 102


geometry [38] and their generalizations to filtered manifolds in [68, 171]. An instance of such a
sequence is the contact complex of Rumin [170]. As a result, to each generalized BGG sequence
we can associate a spectral triple satisfying Condition (H), and so the results of this monograph
apply verbatim to those spectral triples. The formulas that we obtain are similar to those for
sub-Laplacians above. They can be expressed in terms of a spectral density which is computed at
each point as in (11.14) by using the fundamental solution for the heat equation associated with
some model differential operator at that point (see [154] in the case of the contact complex).
Remark 11.13. It is natural to ask for sub-Riemannian versions of the Cwikel-type estimates
we have in the Riemannian setting (cf. Chapter 8). A pointer to this direction is provided by
the analogues of those estimates for graded nilpotent Lie groups in [138]. It is expected that
a localization argument should allow us to extend these estimates at least to nilmanifolds and
contact manifolds, since such manifolds are locally equivalent to graded nilpotent Lie groups. If
we were to have such estimates, then Condition (Cr) would be satisfied, with

4. Contact manifolds
It is worth specializing the above results to contact manifolds, since in this case the above
considerations are simpler and can be made fully explicit.
From now on we let (M 2n+1, θ) be a compact (orientable) contact manifold, where θ is a
globally defined contact form on M, so that the 2-form dθ is non-degenerate on the contact
distribution H = ker θ.
Here H is a hyperplane bundle, and so we have a step 2 filtration
0 ⊆H ⊆TM. In particular, the sR dimension is N = 2n + 2.
Let J be a calibrated almost complex structure on H, i.e., a smooth section of End(H) such
that J2 = −1 and gH(X, Y ) = dθ(X, JY ) is a sub-Riemannian metric on H. We extend gθ to the
Riemannian metric on M given by
(11.22)
˜gH(X, Y ) = dθ(X, JY ) + θ(X)θ(Y ),
X, Y ∈TxM, x ∈M.
We denote by νθ the corresponding Riemannian measure. This is also the Popp measure associated
with (H, gH) in the sense of [149] (see Eq. (11.25) below). We then let ∆H be the corresponding
sub-Laplacian.
In addition, we denote by X0 the Reeb vector field of θ, i.e., the unique (real) vector field
such that θ(X0) = 1 and ιX0dθ = 0. We also let {X1, . . . , X2n} be a local orthonormal frame
of H such that Xn+j = JXj, j = 1, . . . , n.
This ensures that {X1, . . . , X2n, X0} is a graded
tangent frame and the corresponding coframe of T ∗M is of the form {θ1, . . . , θ2n, θ}. Note that,

Remark 11.16. As mentioned in Remark 11.7, the integration formula (11.15) is known
(see [114, 153]). However, Theorem 11.15 provides a stronger form of measurability. The inte-
gration formula (11.15) seems to be new.
Remark 11.17. As mentioned in Remark 11.13, it is expected to have Cwikel-type estimates
for contact manifolds. This would allow us to extend the semiclassical Weyl law (11.28) to (real-
valued) potentials V in L

Remark 11.18 (Step 2 Case). The above considerations in the contact case can be extended
to all step 2 sub-Riemannian manifolds, since for step 2 graded nilpotent Lie groups the funda-
mental solution of the heat equation can be computed explicitly as well (see, e.g., [13, 14] for
the codimension 1 case). In this setting, the tangent group GM(x) may vary with x, and so in
general γ∆H(x) need not be constant. However, it still can be computed explicitly. Codimension
1 examples include Cauchy-Riemann manifolds of hypersurface type and even-dimensional con-
tact manifolds in the sense of [149]. Higher codimension examples include contact quaternionic
manifolds [18] and Cartesian products of contact manifolds. We refer to [45], and the references
therein, for a more detailed discussion of examples of step 2 sub-Riemannian manifolds.
Remark 11.19 (Kohn Laplacian). The above considerations can be further extended to the
Kohn Laplacian [110, 112] acting on (p, q)-forms on a Cauchy-Riemann manifolds of hypersurface
type under the so-called Y (q)-condition.The fact that the square root of the Kohn Laplacian yields
a spectral triple satisfying Condition (H) follows from the results of [14, 154].
5. Extension to singular sub-Riemannian manifolds
It is expected that the semiclassical Weyl law and integration formulas provided by Theo-
rem 11.3 and Theorem 11.6 can be extended to singular sub-Riemannian manifolds, i.e., when
we drop the equiregularity condition.
This means that the rank of the distributions Hw(x0),
w = 1, . . . , r, in (11.1) may vary as x0 ranges over M.
First, we still have spectral triple (C∞(M), L2
ν(M), √∆H). This can be seen by applying the
results of [6] to the heat operator ∆H +∂t. It admits a parametrix in the class of pseudodifferential
operators in [6]. Arguing as in [67, 154] we then can show that √∆H is pseudodifferential operator
of order 1 in this class of pseudodifferential operators. Such operators commute with functions
modulo operators of order 0. It then follows that the commutators [√∆H, f], f ∈C∞(M), and
so (C∞(M), L2
ν(M), √∆H) is a spectral triple.
100


## Page 105


The validity of Condition (H) is a bit more subtle. It is ensured by the local Weyl laws of [50,
Theorem 7.1] for equisingular sub-Riemannian manifolds. Roughly speaking this means that the
sub-Riemannian structure induces a regular filtered structure on its singular set (see [50] for the
precise definition). For instance, the Baouendi-Grushin and Martinet examples fall on this class
of sub-Riemannian manifolds (see [50]).
In general, the singular set and the regular set have different sR-dimensions. The leading
terms in the local Weyl laws is then provided by the greatest of these sR-dimensions, which then
gives the degree of summability of our spectral triple. In case this the sR-dimension of the singular
set, then this yields to a singular Weyl measure that is supported on the singular set. If the two
srR-dimensions agrees, then the leading term has a logarithmic terms, and so this falls out of the
scope of this monograph.
We illustrate the type of results we get in the special case of the Baouendi-Grushin example1
on the 2-torus T2, with
(11.29)
H = ⟨X, Y ⟩,
∆H = X2 + Y 2,
where
(11.30)
X := ∂x,
Y := 2(1 −cos x)∂y.
The singular set is S = {0} × T. Near x = 0 we have Y = x2∂y + O(x4).Thus,
(11.31)
H1 = ⟨X, Y ⟩,
H2 = ⟨X, Y, [X, Y ]⟩,
H3 = ⟨X, Y, [X, Y ], [X, [X, Y ]]⟩.
In particular, on T2 \ S, we have H1 = H2 = H3 = T(T2 \ S), while on S we have
H1|T S = H2|T S = 0,
H3|T S = TS.
Thus, the sR-dimension of T2 \S is 2, while S has sR-dimension 3. In this example the local Weyl
law [50, Theorem 7.1] and the translation invariance with respect to y ensure that Condition (H)
holds with p = 3, and
(11.32)
τ(f) = c

by taking N = 3 and replacing the Weyl measure γ(∆H)dv(x) by the singular measure cδS.

6. Appendix: Connes’ Trace Theorem for ΨHDOs
Near the end of the completion of this manuscript, McDonald [131] posted on arXiv a version
of Connes’ trace theorem for the ΨHDO calculus. In this appendix, we compare McDonald’s result
with the integration formulas provided by Theorem 11.6.

The notion of measurability in Proposition 11.25 is in terms of all normalized traces on L1,∞
is often called universal measurability (see, e.g., [129]). It is a stronger property than strong mea-
surability. However, it is neither stronger nor weaker than spectral measurability. For one thing,
there are universally measurable operators that are not spectrally mesurable. For the other thing,
there are spectrally measurable operators that are not universally measurable (see, e.g., [158]).
Therefore, although Proposition 11.25 covers a larger class of operators and recapsulates the inte-
gration formula (11.20), it does not provide the spectral measurability asserted by Theorem 11.6.
In addition, we cannot deduce from the trace theorem (11.35) the integration formula (11.21) for
the absolute value |∆−N/4
H

H
|, since that operator need not be a ΨHDO.
In any case the conjecture mentioned in Remark 11.2 remains open for general ΨHDOs (see
the discussion on this in [131]).
